I’ll look at the containers or the empty bag and think, “How did I do that again?” So, yeah, that’s why I’m here. It’s been scaring me a little.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty frequent. I started trying to write it down in a journal because I honestly didn’t trust my memory about it, and when I look back, there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. And most weeks it’s not just once—it’s several nights.\n\nI think I was telling myself it was more occasional than it really was, but seeing it written down made it harder to explain away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. It’s not just like, “Oh, I ate too much, I’ll do better tomorrow.” It feels like this heavy shame that just sits on me afterward. I’ll be looking at the wrappers or the takeout containers, and I feel embarrassed even though no one else is there to see it.\n\nAnd it affects what I do afterward, too. I’ve stopped inviting people over because I’m afraid they’ll notice things, or I just feel too ashamed to have anyone in my space after I’ve done that. Even little routines I used to count on in the evening—like yoga, or going out to do gardening chores—I’ll skip them because I’m so preoccupied with what happened. I just kind of shut down and don’t want to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I decide to eat too much and then regret it. It feels like once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips, and even while it’s happening I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop opening things, you don’t need any more, and my hands just… keep going.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just finish this one thing and then close the container,” but then I’m already reaching for something else. Or I’ll stand up like I’m going to put it away, and somehow I end up taking it back with me. It feels almost automatic, like I’m watching myself do it and I’m not the one in charge. That sounds dramatic, but that is really what it feels like in the moment. I can be completely aware that I want to stop, and still not be able to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food usually happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’ve tracked that part carefully enough. I mean, when I think of them, I picture nighttime because that’s when I’m usually alone and everything is quiet, so those stand out more in my mind. But I don’t want to say they only happen at night, because I haven’t really been paying attention to the time of day in a precise way.\n\nIn the journal I was mostly writing down that it happened, not always when it happened. So… I guess the honest answer is that I don’t know if there’s a strict pattern. Night is what I notice more, but I’m not sure I could say it never happens during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly. Faster than I think I even realize in the moment.\n\nIt’s like I’m not sitting down and enjoying food the way a normal meal would be. A lot of times I’m standing at the dining room table, with the microwave going or still warm, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just… eating. One bite is barely swallowed before I’m already reaching for the next thing. I don’t really taste it after the first bite or two. It’s more like I’m trying to get it in before I can think too much, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll sometimes remember it in these little flashes—like opening something, heating something, taking another bite—but I don’t remember actually savoring it. It feels rushed, almost frantic. Like my body is ahead of my brain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, partly because I kind of avoid the scale. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it’s more that I don’t want to see the number and then have it ruin my whole day.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but I can’t say whether that’s a “significant” change or just me being more aware of my body. Some days things feel tighter, and then other days I tell myself maybe I’m imagining it or maybe it’s bloating or just the way something was washed. So I don’t think I can give you a clear number or even a confident yes or no.\n\nIf you mean, like, have I noticed a dramatic change over the past few months, I don’t know that I can say that. I just haven’t kept track in a way that would make me sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, maybe the first bite, if it’s something I’d normally like, I might notice it for a second. But once I’m in that… whatever that mode is, I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything.\n\nIt’s not like sitting down with a plate and thinking, “This is good.” It’s more like I’m standing there at the dining room table, the microwave still going or something still hot from it, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just moving from one thing to the next. I’ll be swallowing one bite while my hand is already reaching for the next. So the taste kind of disappears. It’s just getting it down quickly. I don’t feel like I’m enjoying it in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I’m not sure I would call it “criticize,” exactly, because it’s not always like I’m sitting there making a list of insults at myself or anything that organized.\n\nI do usually feel bad afterward, though. Like there’s this heavy feeling of, “Why did I do that?” or “What was that about?” But whether that counts as criticizing myself, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s more just embarrassment or wanting to avoid thinking about it. I’ll kind of clean up or put things away and try to act like it didn’t happen, even to myself.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I don’t know if I’m using the right words. I don’t always notice exactly what I’m saying to myself afterward, but it definitely doesn’t feel kind.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think very negatively about yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate that from what I already said, if that makes sense. “Very negatively” feels like a strong phrase, and I don’t know if I’m always aware of the exact words in my head afterward.\n\nSometimes it’s less like a clear thought and more like this… sinking feeling. Like I don’t want to be in my own skin for a minute, or I don’t want to look too closely at what just happened. I might think something like, “Why can’t I just be normal about this?” but then I try to shut it down pretty quickly because if I stay with it, it feels awful.\n\nSo, I guess there are negative thoughts, yes, but I don’t know how to measure whether they’re “very” negative or how often. I’m not sitting there calmly evaluating myself afterward. It’s more messy than that. I usually just want to move on and not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. It really does feel like I’m not in control once it starts.\n\nI can be standing there, and part of me is completely aware of what’s happening. Like I’ll have this thought, almost like I’m talking to myself: “Taylor, stop. Put it away. Close the container. Walk away.” And then my hands are still doing the opposite. I’m opening another container, or going back to the bag, or getting something else out, even while I’m thinking I don’t want to keep doing this.\n\nIt feels like this awful switch flips. Before it starts, I might have some choice, or at least it feels like I do. But once I cross that line, it’s like the reasonable part of me is watching from a few steps back, trying to get my attention, and my body just keeps going. I know that probably sounds strange, because of course it’s still me doing it, but in the moment it honestly doesn’t feel like I’m the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually, yes. I wish I could say I stop before that point, but a lot of the time I don’t.\n\nIt’s not just “full” like after a big dinner. It’s more like my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight, and sometimes it actually hurts. And even then, I don’t always stop at the first sign of that feeling. I keep going past where any reasonable person would say, “Okay, that’s enough.” Then afterward I’m just sitting there kind of frozen on the couch, trying not to move too much because moving makes me feel worse.\n\nThere have been times where even the idea of walking upstairs feels uncomfortable, like I’m aware of every step because my stomach feels so heavy and painful. So yes, uncomfortably full is definitely the right phrase. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s usually how far it goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing things like vomiting, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t… I don’t think I do those things in a deliberate way, like a planned “now I have to undo it” kind of way.\n\nVomiting, no, that’s not something I make myself do. Laxatives… no, not as a regular thing or as some kind of compensation. And with exercise, I mean, I can get into a mindset where I think, “I should move more,” or “I need to get back on track,” but I wouldn’t say I go exercise excessively right after to make up for it. At least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nMostly what happens afterward is more like I feel awful and uncomfortable and embarrassed, and I sort of retreat. I might tell myself I’ll be stricter later or do better tomorrow, but I don’t usually turn around and do something drastic like vomiting or laxatives. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: I mean… the large amount part, yes, absolutely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in.\n\nIt’s usually in the evenings. I’ll stop and get takeout, but not just like a normal dinner. It’ll be more like a family-size order, something that’s clearly meant for more than one person, and then I’ll still go into the store and add chips or bakery things or whatever looks good in the moment. And then I get home and eat most of it myself, sometimes before I even really seem to register how much is gone.\n\nIt’s strange because if you put it all out on the counter at the beginning, it would look like too much. Like, I know that. But in the moment it doesn’t land that way. I’ll just keep going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I notice the containers are almost empty or the bag is mostly gone, and I’m like… how did I get here again?\n\nSo yes, I do have episodes where I eat a really large amount in a short time. The “without feeling guilty or ashamed” part is harder for me to say yes to, because it’s not like I’m relaxed and fine afterward. But the eating a lot, quickly, mostly by myself before it fully sinks in — yes, that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time and feel okay about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I ever really feel okay about it afterward. Or if there’s a moment where I feel numb, it’s not “okay” exactly. It’s more like I’m kind of checked out for a little while.\n\nIf I’ve eaten what I know is a lot in a short amount of time, I usually don’t sit there feeling satisfied like, “That was nice, I enjoyed that.” It’s more complicated and heavier than that. Even if the food tasted good at the beginning, afterward I’m usually aware of what just happened, and I start going over it in my head. Like, why did I do that again, why couldn’t I stop earlier, why did I buy all that in the first place.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I feel okay afterward. Maybe during the first few minutes I’m not fully thinking about it, but once it catches up with me, I don’t feel fine about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I’ll leave work and tell myself I’m just picking up dinner, but then I’ll end up buying, like, a family-size takeout order—way more than one person needs—and then I’ll stop at the store too and get chips or bakery things, like pastries or cookies or whatever looks good in the moment.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s like I just… start eating, and I don’t even really register how much is disappearing until most of it is gone. I started trying to write it down in a journal because I honestly didn’t trust my memory about it, and when I look back, there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. And most weeks it’s not just once—it’s several nights.\n\nI think I was telling myself it was more occasional than it really was, but seeing it written down made it harder to explain away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. It’s not just like, “Oh, I ate too much, I’ll do better tomorrow.” It feels like this heavy shame that just sits on me afterward. It feels like once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips, and even while it’s happening I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop opening things, you don’t need any more, and my hands just… keep going.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just finish this one thing and then close the container,” but then I’m already reaching for something else. A lot of times I’m standing at the dining room table, with the microwave going or still warm, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just… eating. One bite is barely swallowed before I’m already reaching for the next thing. I don’t really taste it after the first bite or two. It’s more like I’m trying to get it in before I can think too much, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll sometimes remember it in these little flashes—like opening something, heating something, taking another bite—but I don’t remember actually savoring it. Some days things feel tighter, and then other days I tell myself maybe I’m imagining it or maybe it’s bloating or just the way something was washed. So I don’t think I can give you a clear number or even a confident yes or no.\n\nIf you mean, like, have I noticed a dramatic change over the past few months, I don’t know that I can say that. I just haven’t kept track in a way that would make me sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, maybe the first bite, if it’s something I’d normally like, I might notice it for a second. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in.\n\nIt’s usually in the evenings. I’ll stop and get takeout, but not just like a normal dinner. It’ll be more like a family-size order, something that’s clearly meant for more than one person, and then I’ll still go into the store and add chips or bakery things or whatever looks good in the moment. And then I get home and eat most of it myself, sometimes before I even really seem to register how much is gone.\n\nIt’s strange because if you put it all out on the counter at the beginning, it would look like too much. Like, I know that. But in the moment it doesn’t land that way. I’ll just keep going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I notice the containers are almost empty or the bag is mostly gone, and I’m like… how did I get here again?\n\nSo yes, I do have episodes where I eat a really large amount in a short time. The “without feeling guilty or ashamed” part is harder for me to say yes to, because it’s not like I’m relaxed and fine afterward. But the eating a lot, quickly, mostly by myself before it fully sinks in — yes, that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time and feel okay about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I ever really feel okay about it afterward. Or if there’s a moment where I feel numb, it’s not “okay” exactly. It’s more like I’m kind of checked out for a little while.\n\nIf I’ve eaten what I know is a lot in a short amount of time, I usually don’t sit there feeling satisfied like, “That was nice, I enjoyed that.” It’s more complicated and heavier than that. Even if the food tasted good at the beginning, afterward I’m usually aware of what just happened, and I start going over it in my head. Like, why did I do that again, why couldn’t I stop earlier, why did I buy all that in the first place.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I feel okay afterward. Maybe during the first few minutes I’m not fully thinking about it, but once it catches up with me, I don’t feel fine about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that you're not really interested in the food itself when you're having one of these episodes of eating a large amount?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Rapid_Eating\nmanifestation: \"I eat so fast during those times that I barely taste anything, just standing at the dining room table with the microwave and boxes around me, swallowing one bite while I am already reaching for the next.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s true a lot of the time. Not completely at the very beginning maybe, because sometimes I do want a specific thing, or it sounds good when I’m buying it. But once I’m actually in it, it’s not like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.

I’ll be standing at the dining room table with the boxes and wrappers around me, and the microwave going or just finished, and I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything. It’s almost mechanical. I’ll take a bite and before I’ve even really swallowed it, I’m already reaching for the next thing, opening another container, grabbing another handful, whatever it is.

And afterward I’ll realize I don’t even remember what half of it tasted like. I remember the motion of it more than the food, if that makes sense. Like standing there, going from one thing to the next, swallowing quickly, not really pausing. So yes, during those episodes, the food itself almost becomes secondary. It’s not like normal eating where I’m paying attention to flavor or whether I’m enjoying it. It’s just… fast. Too fast.

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially in the evenings. I’ll leave work and tell myself I’m just picking up dinner, but then I’ll end up buying, like, a family-size takeout order—way more than one person needs—and then I’ll stop at the store too and get chips or bakery things, like pastries or cookies or whatever looks good in the moment.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s like I just… start eating, and I don’t even really register how much is disappearing until most of it is gone. It’s embarrassing to say, but I can eat the majority of all of that by myself before it even hits me. I’ll look at the containers or the empty bag and think, “How did I do that again?” So, yeah, that’s why I’m here. It’s been scaring me a little.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty frequent. I started trying to write it down in a journal because I honestly didn’t trust my memory about it, and when I look back, there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. And most weeks it’s not just once—it’s several nights.\n\nI think I was telling myself it was more occasional than it really was, but seeing it written down made it harder to explain away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. It’s not just like, “Oh, I ate too much, I’ll do better tomorrow.” It feels like this heavy shame that just sits on me afterward. I’ll be looking at the wrappers or the takeout containers, and I feel embarrassed even though no one else is there to see it.\n\nAnd it affects what I do afterward, too. I’ve stopped inviting people over because I’m afraid they’ll notice things, or I just feel too ashamed to have anyone in my space after I’ve done that. Even little routines I used to count on in the evening—like yoga, or going out to do gardening chores—I’ll skip them because I’m so preoccupied with what happened. I just kind of shut down and don’t want to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I decide to eat too much and then regret it. It feels like once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips, and even while it’s happening I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop opening things, you don’t need any more, and my hands just… keep going.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just finish this one thing and then close the container,” but then I’m already reaching for something else. Or I’ll stand up like I’m going to put it away, and somehow I end up taking it back with me. It feels almost automatic, like I’m watching myself do it and I’m not the one in charge. That sounds dramatic, but that is really what it feels like in the moment. I can be completely aware that I want to stop, and still not be able to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food usually happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’ve tracked that part carefully enough. I mean, when I think of them, I picture nighttime because that’s when I’m usually alone and everything is quiet, so those stand out more in my mind. But I don’t want to say they only happen at night, because I haven’t really been paying attention to the time of day in a precise way.\n\nIn the journal I was mostly writing down that it happened, not always when it happened. So… I guess the honest answer is that I don’t know if there’s a strict pattern. Night is what I notice more, but I’m not sure I could say it never happens during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly. Faster than I think I even realize in the moment.\n\nIt’s like I’m not sitting down and enjoying food the way a normal meal would be. A lot of times I’m standing at the dining room table, with the microwave going or still warm, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just… eating. One bite is barely swallowed before I’m already reaching for the next thing. I don’t really taste it after the first bite or two. It’s more like I’m trying to get it in before I can think too much, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll sometimes remember it in these little flashes—like opening something, heating something, taking another bite—but I don’t remember actually savoring it. It feels rushed, almost frantic. Like my body is ahead of my brain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, partly because I kind of avoid the scale. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it’s more that I don’t want to see the number and then have it ruin my whole day.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but I can’t say whether that’s a “significant” change or just me being more aware of my body. Some days things feel tighter, and then other days I tell myself maybe I’m imagining it or maybe it’s bloating or just the way something was washed. So I don’t think I can give you a clear number or even a confident yes or no.\n\nIf you mean, like, have I noticed a dramatic change over the past few months, I don’t know that I can say that. I just haven’t kept track in a way that would make me sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, maybe the first bite, if it’s something I’d normally like, I might notice it for a second. But once I’m in that… whatever that mode is, I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything.\n\nIt’s not like sitting down with a plate and thinking, “This is good.” It’s more like I’m standing there at the dining room table, the microwave still going or something still hot from it, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just moving from one thing to the next. I’ll be swallowing one bite while my hand is already reaching for the next. So the taste kind of disappears. It’s just getting it down quickly. I don’t feel like I’m enjoying it in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I’m not sure I would call it “criticize,” exactly, because it’s not always like I’m sitting there making a list of insults at myself or anything that organized.\n\nI do usually feel bad afterward, though. Like there’s this heavy feeling of, “Why did I do that?” or “What was that about?” But whether that counts as criticizing myself, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s more just embarrassment or wanting to avoid thinking about it. I’ll kind of clean up or put things away and try to act like it didn’t happen, even to myself.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I don’t know if I’m using the right words. I don’t always notice exactly what I’m saying to myself afterward, but it definitely doesn’t feel kind.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think very negatively about yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate that from what I already said, if that makes sense. “Very negatively” feels like a strong phrase, and I don’t know if I’m always aware of the exact words in my head afterward.\n\nSometimes it’s less like a clear thought and more like this… sinking feeling. Like I don’t want to be in my own skin for a minute, or I don’t want to look too closely at what just happened. I might think something like, “Why can’t I just be normal about this?” but then I try to shut it down pretty quickly because if I stay with it, it feels awful.\n\nSo, I guess there are negative thoughts, yes, but I don’t know how to measure whether they’re “very” negative or how often. I’m not sitting there calmly evaluating myself afterward. It’s more messy than that. I usually just want to move on and not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. It really does feel like I’m not in control once it starts.\n\nI can be standing there, and part of me is completely aware of what’s happening. Like I’ll have this thought, almost like I’m talking to myself: “Taylor, stop. Put it away. Close the container. Walk away.” And then my hands are still doing the opposite. I’m opening another container, or going back to the bag, or getting something else out, even while I’m thinking I don’t want to keep doing this.\n\nIt feels like this awful switch flips. Before it starts, I might have some choice, or at least it feels like I do. But once I cross that line, it’s like the reasonable part of me is watching from a few steps back, trying to get my attention, and my body just keeps going. I know that probably sounds strange, because of course it’s still me doing it, but in the moment it honestly doesn’t feel like I’m the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually, yes. I wish I could say I stop before that point, but a lot of the time I don’t.\n\nIt’s not just “full” like after a big dinner. It’s more like my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight, and sometimes it actually hurts. And even then, I don’t always stop at the first sign of that feeling. I keep going past where any reasonable person would say, “Okay, that’s enough.” Then afterward I’m just sitting there kind of frozen on the couch, trying not to move too much because moving makes me feel worse.\n\nThere have been times where even the idea of walking upstairs feels uncomfortable, like I’m aware of every step because my stomach feels so heavy and painful. So yes, uncomfortably full is definitely the right phrase. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s usually how far it goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing things like vomiting, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t… I don’t think I do those things in a deliberate way, like a planned “now I have to undo it” kind of way.\n\nVomiting, no, that’s not something I make myself do. Laxatives… no, not as a regular thing or as some kind of compensation. And with exercise, I mean, I can get into a mindset where I think, “I should move more,” or “I need to get back on track,” but I wouldn’t say I go exercise excessively right after to make up for it. At least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nMostly what happens afterward is more like I feel awful and uncomfortable and embarrassed, and I sort of retreat. I might tell myself I’ll be stricter later or do better tomorrow, but I don’t usually turn around and do something drastic like vomiting or laxatives. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: I mean… the large amount part, yes, absolutely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in.\n\nIt’s usually in the evenings. I’ll stop and get takeout, but not just like a normal dinner. It’ll be more like a family-size order, something that’s clearly meant for more than one person, and then I’ll still go into the store and add chips or bakery things or whatever looks good in the moment. And then I get home and eat most of it myself, sometimes before I even really seem to register how much is gone.\n\nIt’s strange because if you put it all out on the counter at the beginning, it would look like too much. Like, I know that. But in the moment it doesn’t land that way. I’ll just keep going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I notice the containers are almost empty or the bag is mostly gone, and I’m like… how did I get here again?\n\nSo yes, I do have episodes where I eat a really large amount in a short time. The “without feeling guilty or ashamed” part is harder for me to say yes to, because it’s not like I’m relaxed and fine afterward. But the eating a lot, quickly, mostly by myself before it fully sinks in — yes, that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time and feel okay about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I ever really feel okay about it afterward. Or if there’s a moment where I feel numb, it’s not “okay” exactly. It’s more like I’m kind of checked out for a little while.\n\nIf I’ve eaten what I know is a lot in a short amount of time, I usually don’t sit there feeling satisfied like, “That was nice, I enjoyed that.” It’s more complicated and heavier than that. Even if the food tasted good at the beginning, afterward I’m usually aware of what just happened, and I start going over it in my head. Like, why did I do that again, why couldn’t I stop earlier, why did I buy all that in the first place.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I feel okay afterward. Maybe during the first few minutes I’m not fully thinking about it, but once it catches up with me, I don’t feel fine about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're not really interested in the food itself when you're having one of these episodes of eating a large amount?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s true a lot of the time. Not completely at the very beginning maybe, because sometimes I do want a specific thing, or it sounds good when I’m buying it. But once I’m actually in it, it’s not like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nI’ll be standing at the dining room table with the boxes and wrappers around me, and the microwave going or just finished, and I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything. So yes, during those episodes, the food itself almost becomes secondary. It’s not like normal eating where I’m paying attention to flavor or whether I’m enjoying it. It’s just… fast. Too fast.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I usually just want to move on and not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. It really does feel like I’m not in control once it starts.\n\nI can be standing there, and part of me is completely aware of what’s happening. Like I’ll have this thought, almost like I’m talking to myself: “Taylor, stop. Put it away. Close the container. Walk away.” And then my hands are still doing the opposite. I’m opening another container, or going back to the bag, or getting something else out, even while I’m thinking I don’t want to keep doing this.\n\nIt feels like this awful switch flips. Before it starts, I might have some choice, or at least it feels like I do. But once I cross that line, it’s like the reasonable part of me is watching from a few steps back, trying to get my attention, and my body just keeps going. I know that probably sounds strange, because of course it’s still me doing it, but in the moment it honestly doesn’t feel like I’m the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually, yes. I wish I could say I stop before that point, but a lot of the time I don’t.\n\nIt’s not just “full” like after a big dinner. It’s more like my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight, and sometimes it actually hurts. And even then, I don’t always stop at the first sign of that feeling. I keep going past where any reasonable person would say, “Okay, that’s enough.” Then afterward I’m just sitting there kind of frozen on the couch, trying not to move too much because moving makes me feel worse.\n\nThere have been times where even the idea of walking upstairs feels uncomfortable, like I’m aware of every step because my stomach feels so heavy and painful. So yes, uncomfortably full is definitely the right phrase. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s usually how far it goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing things like vomiting, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t… I don’t think I do those things in a deliberate way, like a planned “now I have to undo it” kind of way.\n\nVomiting, no, that’s not something I make myself do. Laxatives… no, not as a regular thing or as some kind of compensation. And with exercise, I mean, I can get into a mindset where I think, “I should move more,” or “I need to get back on track,” but I wouldn’t say I go exercise excessively right after to make up for it. At least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nMostly what happens afterward is more like I feel awful and uncomfortable and embarrassed, and I sort of retreat. I might tell myself I’ll be stricter later or do better tomorrow, but I don’t usually turn around and do something drastic like vomiting or laxatives. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: I mean… the large amount part, yes, absolutely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in.\n\nIt’s usually in the evenings. I’ll stop and get takeout, but not just like a normal dinner. It’ll be more like a family-size order, something that’s clearly meant for more than one person, and then I’ll still go into the store and add chips or bakery things or whatever looks good in the moment. And then I get home and eat most of it myself, sometimes before I even really seem to register how much is gone.\n\nIt’s strange because if you put it all out on the counter at the beginning, it would look like too much. Like, I know that. But in the moment it doesn’t land that way. I’ll just keep going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I notice the containers are almost empty or the bag is mostly gone, and I’m like… how did I get here again?\n\nSo yes, I do have episodes where I eat a really large amount in a short time. The “without feeling guilty or ashamed” part is harder for me to say yes to, because it’s not like I’m relaxed and fine afterward. But the eating a lot, quickly, mostly by myself before it fully sinks in — yes, that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time and feel okay about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I ever really feel okay about it afterward. Or if there’s a moment where I feel numb, it’s not “okay” exactly. It’s more like I’m kind of checked out for a little while.\n\nIf I’ve eaten what I know is a lot in a short amount of time, I usually don’t sit there feeling satisfied like, “That was nice, I enjoyed that.” It’s more complicated and heavier than that. Even if the food tasted good at the beginning, afterward I’m usually aware of what just happened, and I start going over it in my head. Like, why did I do that again, why couldn’t I stop earlier, why did I buy all that in the first place.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I feel okay afterward. Maybe during the first few minutes I’m not fully thinking about it, but once it catches up with me, I don’t feel fine about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're not really interested in the food itself when you're having one of these episodes of eating a large amount?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s true a lot of the time. Not completely at the very beginning maybe, because sometimes I do want a specific thing, or it sounds good when I’m buying it. But once I’m actually in it, it’s not like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nI’ll be standing at the dining room table with the boxes and wrappers around me, and the microwave going or just finished, and I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything. It’s almost mechanical. I’ll take a bite and before I’ve even really swallowed it, I’m already reaching for the next thing, opening another container, grabbing another handful, whatever it is.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll realize I don’t even remember what half of it tasted like. I remember the motion of it more than the food, if that makes sense. Like standing there, going from one thing to the next, swallowing quickly, not really pausing. So yes, during those episodes, the food itself almost becomes secondary. It’s not like normal eating where I’m paying attention to flavor or whether I’m enjoying it. It’s just… fast. Too fast.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it in a very organized way, so I don’t want to say a number and be wrong.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially in the evenings. I’ll leave work and tell myself I’m just picking up dinner, but then I’ll end up buying, like, a family-size takeout order—way more than one person needs—and then I’ll stop at the store too and get chips or bakery things, like pastries or cookies or whatever looks good in the moment.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s like I just… start eating, and I don’t even really register how much is disappearing until most of it is gone. It’s embarrassing to say, but I can eat the majority of all of that by myself before it even hits me. I’ll look at the containers or the empty bag and think, “How did I do that again?” So, yeah, that’s why I’m here. It’s been scaring me a little.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty frequent. I started trying to write it down in a journal because I honestly didn’t trust my memory about it, and when I look back, there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. And most weeks it’s not just once—it’s several nights.\n\nI think I was telling myself it was more occasional than it really was, but seeing it written down made it harder to explain away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. It’s not just like, “Oh, I ate too much, I’ll do better tomorrow.” It feels like this heavy shame that just sits on me afterward. I’ll be looking at the wrappers or the takeout containers, and I feel embarrassed even though no one else is there to see it.\n\nAnd it affects what I do afterward, too. I’ve stopped inviting people over because I’m afraid they’ll notice things, or I just feel too ashamed to have anyone in my space after I’ve done that. Even little routines I used to count on in the evening—like yoga, or going out to do gardening chores—I’ll skip them because I’m so preoccupied with what happened. I just kind of shut down and don’t want to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I decide to eat too much and then regret it. It feels like once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips, and even while it’s happening I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop opening things, you don’t need any more, and my hands just… keep going.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just finish this one thing and then close the container,” but then I’m already reaching for something else. Or I’ll stand up like I’m going to put it away, and somehow I end up taking it back with me. It feels almost automatic, like I’m watching myself do it and I’m not the one in charge. That sounds dramatic, but that is really what it feels like in the moment. I can be completely aware that I want to stop, and still not be able to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food usually happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’ve tracked that part carefully enough. I mean, when I think of them, I picture nighttime because that’s when I’m usually alone and everything is quiet, so those stand out more in my mind. But I don’t want to say they only happen at night, because I haven’t really been paying attention to the time of day in a precise way.\n\nIn the journal I was mostly writing down that it happened, not always when it happened. So… I guess the honest answer is that I don’t know if there’s a strict pattern. Night is what I notice more, but I’m not sure I could say it never happens during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly. Faster than I think I even realize in the moment.\n\nIt’s like I’m not sitting down and enjoying food the way a normal meal would be. A lot of times I’m standing at the dining room table, with the microwave going or still warm, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just… eating. One bite is barely swallowed before I’m already reaching for the next thing. I don’t really taste it after the first bite or two. It’s more like I’m trying to get it in before I can think too much, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll sometimes remember it in these little flashes—like opening something, heating something, taking another bite—but I don’t remember actually savoring it. It feels rushed, almost frantic. Like my body is ahead of my brain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, partly because I kind of avoid the scale. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it’s more that I don’t want to see the number and then have it ruin my whole day.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but I can’t say whether that’s a “significant” change or just me being more aware of my body. Some days things feel tighter, and then other days I tell myself maybe I’m imagining it or maybe it’s bloating or just the way something was washed. So I don’t think I can give you a clear number or even a confident yes or no.\n\nIf you mean, like, have I noticed a dramatic change over the past few months, I don’t know that I can say that. I just haven’t kept track in a way that would make me sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, maybe the first bite, if it’s something I’d normally like, I might notice it for a second. But once I’m in that… whatever that mode is, I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything.\n\nIt’s not like sitting down with a plate and thinking, “This is good.” It’s more like I’m standing there at the dining room table, the microwave still going or something still hot from it, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just moving from one thing to the next. I’ll be swallowing one bite while my hand is already reaching for the next. So the taste kind of disappears. It’s just getting it down quickly. I don’t feel like I’m enjoying it in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I’m not sure I would call it “criticize,” exactly, because it’s not always like I’m sitting there making a list of insults at myself or anything that organized.\n\nI do usually feel bad afterward, though. Like there’s this heavy feeling of, “Why did I do that?” or “What was that about?” But whether that counts as criticizing myself, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s more just embarrassment or wanting to avoid thinking about it. I’ll kind of clean up or put things away and try to act like it didn’t happen, even to myself.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I don’t know if I’m using the right words. I don’t always notice exactly what I’m saying to myself afterward, but it definitely doesn’t feel kind.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think very negatively about yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate that from what I already said, if that makes sense. “Very negatively” feels like a strong phrase, and I don’t know if I’m always aware of the exact words in my head afterward.\n\nSometimes it’s less like a clear thought and more like this… sinking feeling. Like I don’t want to be in my own skin for a minute, or I don’t want to look too closely at what just happened. I might think something like, “Why can’t I just be normal about this?” but then I try to shut it down pretty quickly because if I stay with it, it feels awful.\n\nSo, I guess there are negative thoughts, yes, but I don’t know how to measure whether they’re “very” negative or how often. I’m not sitting there calmly evaluating myself afterward. It’s more messy than that. I usually just want to move on and not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. It really does feel like I’m not in control once it starts.\n\nI can be standing there, and part of me is completely aware of what’s happening. Like I’ll have this thought, almost like I’m talking to myself: “Taylor, stop. Put it away. Close the container. Walk away.” And then my hands are still doing the opposite. I’m opening another container, or going back to the bag, or getting something else out, even while I’m thinking I don’t want to keep doing this.\n\nIt feels like this awful switch flips. Before it starts, I might have some choice, or at least it feels like I do. But once I cross that line, it’s like the reasonable part of me is watching from a few steps back, trying to get my attention, and my body just keeps going. I know that probably sounds strange, because of course it’s still me doing it, but in the moment it honestly doesn’t feel like I’m the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually, yes. I wish I could say I stop before that point, but a lot of the time I don’t.\n\nIt’s not just “full” like after a big dinner. It’s more like my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight, and sometimes it actually hurts. And even then, I don’t always stop at the first sign of that feeling. I keep going past where any reasonable person would say, “Okay, that’s enough.” Then afterward I’m just sitting there kind of frozen on the couch, trying not to move too much because moving makes me feel worse.\n\nThere have been times where even the idea of walking upstairs feels uncomfortable, like I’m aware of every step because my stomach feels so heavy and painful. So yes, uncomfortably full is definitely the right phrase. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s usually how far it goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing things like vomiting, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t… I don’t think I do those things in a deliberate way, like a planned “now I have to undo it” kind of way.\n\nVomiting, no, that’s not something I make myself do. Laxatives… no, not as a regular thing or as some kind of compensation. And with exercise, I mean, I can get into a mindset where I think, “I should move more,” or “I need to get back on track,” but I wouldn’t say I go exercise excessively right after to make up for it. At least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nMostly what happens afterward is more like I feel awful and uncomfortable and embarrassed, and I sort of retreat. I might tell myself I’ll be stricter later or do better tomorrow, but I don’t usually turn around and do something drastic like vomiting or laxatives. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: I mean… the large amount part, yes, absolutely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in.\n\nIt’s usually in the evenings. I’ll stop and get takeout, but not just like a normal dinner. It’ll be more like a family-size order, something that’s clearly meant for more than one person, and then I’ll still go into the store and add chips or bakery things or whatever looks good in the moment. And then I get home and eat most of it myself, sometimes before I even really seem to register how much is gone.\n\nIt’s strange because if you put it all out on the counter at the beginning, it would look like too much. Like, I know that. But in the moment it doesn’t land that way. I’ll just keep going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I notice the containers are almost empty or the bag is mostly gone, and I’m like… how did I get here again?\n\nSo yes, I do have episodes where I eat a really large amount in a short time. The “without feeling guilty or ashamed” part is harder for me to say yes to, because it’s not like I’m relaxed and fine afterward. But the eating a lot, quickly, mostly by myself before it fully sinks in — yes, that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time and feel okay about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I ever really feel okay about it afterward. Or if there’s a moment where I feel numb, it’s not “okay” exactly. It’s more like I’m kind of checked out for a little while.\n\nIf I’ve eaten what I know is a lot in a short amount of time, I usually don’t sit there feeling satisfied like, “That was nice, I enjoyed that.” It’s more complicated and heavier than that. Even if the food tasted good at the beginning, afterward I’m usually aware of what just happened, and I start going over it in my head. Like, why did I do that again, why couldn’t I stop earlier, why did I buy all that in the first place.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I feel okay afterward. Maybe during the first few minutes I’m not fully thinking about it, but once it catches up with me, I don’t feel fine about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're not really interested in the food itself when you're having one of these episodes of eating a large amount?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s true a lot of the time. Not completely at the very beginning maybe, because sometimes I do want a specific thing, or it sounds good when I’m buying it. But once I’m actually in it, it’s not like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nI’ll be standing at the dining room table with the boxes and wrappers around me, and the microwave going or just finished, and I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything. It’s almost mechanical. I’ll take a bite and before I’ve even really swallowed it, I’m already reaching for the next thing, opening another container, grabbing another handful, whatever it is.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll realize I don’t even remember what half of it tasted like. I remember the motion of it more than the food, if that makes sense. Like standing there, going from one thing to the next, swallowing quickly, not really pausing. So yes, during those episodes, the food itself almost becomes secondary. It’s not like normal eating where I’m paying attention to flavor or whether I’m enjoying it. It’s just… fast. Too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight gain since these episodes of eating large amounts of food started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it in a very organized way, so I don’t want to say a number and be wrong.\n\nI’ve noticed my clothes feel different sometimes, and I get nervous stepping on a scale, so I tend to avoid it. But whether it’s been a “significant” amount since these episodes started… I don’t really know. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually changed from what I’m afraid has changed, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure. I haven’t been keeping close enough track to say clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

But once I’m actually in it, it’s not like I’m sitting down and enjoying a meal.\n\nI’ll be standing at the dining room table with the boxes and wrappers around me, and the microwave going or just finished, and I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything. It’s almost mechanical. I’ll take a bite and before I’ve even really swallowed it, I’m already reaching for the next thing, opening another container, grabbing another handful, whatever it is.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll realize I don’t even remember what half of it tasted like. I remember the motion of it more than the food, if that makes sense. Like standing there, going from one thing to the next, swallowing quickly, not really pausing. So yes, during those episodes, the food itself almost becomes secondary. It’s not like normal eating where I’m paying attention to flavor or whether I’m enjoying it. It’s just… fast. Too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight gain since these episodes of eating large amounts of food started?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it in a very organized way, so I don’t want to say a number and be wrong.\n\nI’ve noticed my clothes feel different sometimes, and I get nervous stepping on a scale, so I tend to avoid it. But whether it’s been a “significant” amount since these episodes started… I don’t really know. It’s hard for me to separate what’s actually changed from what I’m afraid has changed, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure. I haven’t been keeping close enough track to say clearly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I… I don’t really know how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I have a specific answer I can give you, like something I know for sure and can point to. It’s not something I’ve been keeping track of in that way.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my eating has gotten really out of hand, especially in the evenings. I’ll leave work and tell myself I’m just picking up dinner, but then I’ll end up buying, like, a family-size takeout order—way more than one person needs—and then I’ll stop at the store too and get chips or bakery things, like pastries or cookies or whatever looks good in the moment.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s like I just… start eating, and I don’t even really register how much is disappearing until most of it is gone. It’s embarrassing to say, but I can eat the majority of all of that by myself before it even hits me. I’ll look at the containers or the empty bag and think, “How did I do that again?” So, yeah, that’s why I’m here. It’s been scaring me a little.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty frequent. I started trying to write it down in a journal because I honestly didn’t trust my memory about it, and when I look back, there’s barely been a week in the last several months where it didn’t happen at least once. And most weeks it’s not just once—it’s several nights.\n\nI think I was telling myself it was more occasional than it really was, but seeing it written down made it harder to explain away.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, honestly. It’s not just like, “Oh, I ate too much, I’ll do better tomorrow.” It feels like this heavy shame that just sits on me afterward. I’ll be looking at the wrappers or the takeout containers, and I feel embarrassed even though no one else is there to see it.\n\nAnd it affects what I do afterward, too. I’ve stopped inviting people over because I’m afraid they’ll notice things, or I just feel too ashamed to have anyone in my space after I’ve done that. Even little routines I used to count on in the evening—like yoga, or going out to do gardening chores—I’ll skip them because I’m so preoccupied with what happened. I just kind of shut down and don’t want to be seen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I decide to eat too much and then regret it. It feels like once I start, there’s this awful switch that flips, and even while it’s happening I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, stop opening things, you don’t need any more, and my hands just… keep going.\n\nLike I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just finish this one thing and then close the container,” but then I’m already reaching for something else. Or I’ll stand up like I’m going to put it away, and somehow I end up taking it back with me. It feels almost automatic, like I’m watching myself do it and I’m not the one in charge. That sounds dramatic, but that is really what it feels like in the moment. I can be completely aware that I want to stop, and still not be able to make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating large amounts of food usually happen at night, or do they also occur during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure I’ve tracked that part carefully enough. I mean, when I think of them, I picture nighttime because that’s when I’m usually alone and everything is quiet, so those stand out more in my mind. But I don’t want to say they only happen at night, because I haven’t really been paying attention to the time of day in a precise way.\n\nIn the journal I was mostly writing down that it happened, not always when it happened. So… I guess the honest answer is that I don’t know if there’s a strict pattern. Night is what I notice more, but I’m not sure I could say it never happens during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly. Faster than I think I even realize in the moment.\n\nIt’s like I’m not sitting down and enjoying food the way a normal meal would be. A lot of times I’m standing at the dining room table, with the microwave going or still warm, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just… eating. One bite is barely swallowed before I’m already reaching for the next thing. I don’t really taste it after the first bite or two. It’s more like I’m trying to get it in before I can think too much, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd afterward I’ll sometimes remember it in these little flashes—like opening something, heating something, taking another bite—but I don’t remember actually savoring it. It feels rushed, almost frantic. Like my body is ahead of my brain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, partly because I kind of avoid the scale. I know that probably sounds evasive, but it’s more that I don’t want to see the number and then have it ruin my whole day.\n\nMy clothes feel different sometimes, but I can’t say whether that’s a “significant” change or just me being more aware of my body. Some days things feel tighter, and then other days I tell myself maybe I’m imagining it or maybe it’s bloating or just the way something was washed. So I don’t think I can give you a clear number or even a confident yes or no.\n\nIf you mean, like, have I noticed a dramatic change over the past few months, I don’t know that I can say that. I just haven’t kept track in a way that would make me sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy the taste of the food while you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, maybe the first bite, if it’s something I’d normally like, I might notice it for a second. But once I’m in that… whatever that mode is, I’m eating so fast that I barely taste anything.\n\nIt’s not like sitting down with a plate and thinking, “This is good.” It’s more like I’m standing there at the dining room table, the microwave still going or something still hot from it, and there are boxes or containers around me, and I’m just moving from one thing to the next. I’ll be swallowing one bite while my hand is already reaching for the next. So the taste kind of disappears. It’s just getting it down quickly. I don’t feel like I’m enjoying it in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I’m not sure I would call it “criticize,” exactly, because it’s not always like I’m sitting there making a list of insults at myself or anything that organized.\n\nI do usually feel bad afterward, though. Like there’s this heavy feeling of, “Why did I do that?” or “What was that about?” But whether that counts as criticizing myself, I’m not sure. Sometimes it’s more just embarrassment or wanting to avoid thinking about it. I’ll kind of clean up or put things away and try to act like it didn’t happen, even to myself.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I don’t know if I’m using the right words. I don’t always notice exactly what I’m saying to myself afterward, but it definitely doesn’t feel kind.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think very negatively about yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate that from what I already said, if that makes sense. “Very negatively” feels like a strong phrase, and I don’t know if I’m always aware of the exact words in my head afterward.\n\nSometimes it’s less like a clear thought and more like this… sinking feeling. Like I don’t want to be in my own skin for a minute, or I don’t want to look too closely at what just happened. I might think something like, “Why can’t I just be normal about this?” but then I try to shut it down pretty quickly because if I stay with it, it feels awful.\n\nSo, I guess there are negative thoughts, yes, but I don’t know how to measure whether they’re “very” negative or how often. I’m not sitting there calmly evaluating myself afterward. It’s more messy than that. I usually just want to move on and not think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. It really does feel like I’m not in control once it starts.\n\nI can be standing there, and part of me is completely aware of what’s happening. Like I’ll have this thought, almost like I’m talking to myself: “Taylor, stop. Put it away. Close the container. Walk away.” And then my hands are still doing the opposite. I’m opening another container, or going back to the bag, or getting something else out, even while I’m thinking I don’t want to keep doing this.\n\nIt feels like this awful switch flips. Before it starts, I might have some choice, or at least it feels like I do. But once I cross that line, it’s like the reasonable part of me is watching from a few steps back, trying to get my attention, and my body just keeps going. I know that probably sounds strange, because of course it’s still me doing it, but in the moment it honestly doesn’t feel like I’m the one in charge.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… usually, yes. I wish I could say I stop before that point, but a lot of the time I don’t.\n\nIt’s not just “full” like after a big dinner. It’s more like my stomach feels tight, like stretched tight, and sometimes it actually hurts. And even then, I don’t always stop at the first sign of that feeling. I keep going past where any reasonable person would say, “Okay, that’s enough.” Then afterward I’m just sitting there kind of frozen on the couch, trying not to move too much because moving makes me feel worse.\n\nThere have been times where even the idea of walking upstairs feels uncomfortable, like I’m aware of every step because my stomach feels so heavy and painful. So yes, uncomfortably full is definitely the right phrase. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s usually how far it goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing things like vomiting, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t… I don’t think I do those things in a deliberate way, like a planned “now I have to undo it” kind of way.\n\nVomiting, no, that’s not something I make myself do. Laxatives… no, not as a regular thing or as some kind of compensation. And with exercise, I mean, I can get into a mindset where I think, “I should move more,” or “I need to get back on track,” but I wouldn’t say I go exercise excessively right after to make up for it. At least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nMostly what happens afterward is more like I feel awful and uncomfortable and embarrassed, and I sort of retreat. I might tell myself I’ll be stricter later or do better tomorrow, but I don’t usually turn around and do something drastic like vomiting or laxatives. So… no, I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time without feeling guilty or ashamed afterward?\n\nPatient: I mean… the large amount part, yes, absolutely. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in.\n\nIt’s usually in the evenings. I’ll stop and get takeout, but not just like a normal dinner. It’ll be more like a family-size order, something that’s clearly meant for more than one person, and then I’ll still go into the store and add chips or bakery things or whatever looks good in the moment. And then I get home and eat most of it myself, sometimes before I even really seem to register how much is gone.\n\nIt’s strange because if you put it all out on the counter at the beginning, it would look like too much. Like, I know that. But in the moment it doesn’t land that way. I’ll just keep going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I notice the containers are almost empty or the bag is mostly gone, and I’m like… how did I get here again?\n\nSo yes, I do have episodes where I eat a really large amount in a short time. The “without feeling guilty or ashamed” part is harder for me to say yes to, because it’s not like I’m relaxed and fine afterward. But the eating a lot, quickly, mostly by myself before it fully sinks in — yes, that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time and feel okay about it afterward?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I ever really feel okay about it afterward. Or if there’s a moment where I feel numb, it’s not “okay” exactly. It’s more like I’m kind of checked out for a little while.\n\nIf I’ve eaten what I know is a lot in a short amount of time, I usually don’t sit there feeling satisfied like, “That was nice, I enjoyed that.” It’s more complicated and heavier than that. Even if the food tasted good at the beginning, afterward I’m usually aware of what just happened, and I start going over it in my head. Like, why did I do that again, why couldn’t I stop earlier, why did I buy all that in the first place.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say I feel okay afterward. Maybe during the first few minutes I’m not fully thinking about it, but once it catches up with me, I don’t feel fine about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're not really interested in the food itself when you're having one of these episodes of eating a large amount?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s true a lot of the time. Not completely at the very beginning maybe, because sometimes I do want a specific thing, or it sounds good when I’m buying it. It’s almost mechanical.
